140	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
141	\paragraph{Downstream impact.}
142	\label{sec:downstream-impact}
143	Among constraint-removed triples, only 0.49\% match gold labels (``lucky violations''; Appendix~\ref{app:lucky-violations}).
144	Error analysis of 514 false negatives (Qwen3-8B LoRA + S1) confirms only one (0.2\%) was blocked by constraints; the dominant error sources---relation type confusion (Part-of$\leftrightarrow$Used-for) and entity type polysemy---are schema-valid and beyond the reach of type constraints (Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}).

```text
1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.
6	Constraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
7	
8	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
9	\label{sec:beam-results}
10	With LoRA, beam search ($k{=}4$)~\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on both SciERC and DocRED (R-F1 43--47; Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}), yielding two deployment tiers: greedy S1 ($+$10\% latency, DR${<}$0.1\%, CF${\leq}$1.4\%) and beam S1 (1.54$\times$ latency, all violations${\to}$0\%; Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency}).
11	
12	% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200
13	\paragraph{Preliminary 70B-class validation.}
14	\label{sec:70b-results}
15	On Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4), zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets: SciERC 6.41\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 11.24), DocRED 14.98\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 13.05); CF rises to 0.04--3.50\%, comparable to 8B zero-shot CF.
16	% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_ablation (grammar_only, semantic_only, full_s1)
17	A four-way ablation on SciERC reveals scale-dependent layer interaction: semantic-only achieves 84\% DR reduction (6.41\%\,$\to$\,1.01\%), far exceeding the 8B result (24\%; Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}), reflecting stronger internalized JSON generation; grammar remains necessary for RV elimination (0.61\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and CF control (semantic-only CF${=}$76).
18	% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained
19	LoRA confirms composability: DR\,$\to$\,0\% on both datasets (R-F1 44.53, 43.95; $|\Delta| \leq 0.20$\,pp); CF drops to 0.18--1.33\%, consistent with the 8B pattern (\S\ref{sec:composability}).
20	
21	% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc
22	\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}
23	\label{sec:constrained-baselines}
24	Our grammar-only ablation (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same constraints as GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}; vLLM \texttt{guided\_json}~\cite{kwon2023vllm} confirms equivalence (DR${=}$15.26\%, R-F1${=}$8.75; GCD-equiv.\ row, Appendix~\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).
25	The ${\sim}$15\% residual DR is the gap our semantic layer addresses (94\% total reduction).
26	Trie-only constraints without JSON enforcement yield 9--51\% parse failure (Appendix~\ref{app:trie-baseline}).
27	
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	We provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.
5	
6	\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}
7	XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and related systems~\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking.
8	NeuroLogic Decoding~\cite{lu2021neurologic} enforces predicate-logic constraints via beam-level reranking; OntoCoDec applies constraints per-token via forward masking, avoiding beam-width dependence.
9	In code generation, PICARD~\cite{scholak2021picard} and \citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata; PICARD prunes invalid beam hypotheses post-generation, whereas OntoCoDec applies proactive token-level masking before sampling, and maintains a dynamic entity registry across multi-relation outputs rather than single-output SQL queries (\S\ref{sec:composition}).
10	DCCD~\cite{dccd2026} and \citet{nguyen2026thinking} decouple reasoning from constrained generation via multi-pass architectures; OntoCoDec enforces schema semantics within a single forward pass.
11	
12	\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}
13	GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, GUIDEX~\cite{guidex2025}, GenIE~\cite{josifoski2022genie}, TANL~\cite{paolini2021tanl}, REBEL~\cite{cabot2021rebel}, InstructUIE~\cite{wang2023instructuie}, and others~\cite{fei2022lasuie,wang2022deepstruct,lu2022uie,lou2023usm,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure or frame IE as structured generation, though schema compliance gaps persist~\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.
14	GoLLIE and KnowCoder embed schema knowledge via code-style prompts (training-time internalization without runtime enforcement); GoLLIE-7B eliminates DR at severe coverage cost (R-F1${=}$0.0, 46\% empty output), while OntoCoDec achieves R-F1${=}$45.47 at DR${=}$0.09\%~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}.
15	
16	\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}
17	\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation but discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives.
18	EnriCo~\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} uses ASP; IterGen~\cite{ugare2025itergen} backtracks on violations---OntoCoDec uses forward-only masking at $O(|V|)$ per step, avoiding backtracking latency.
19	Grammar-only enforcement (GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only 3\% DR reduction (\S\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}).
20	Extraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\cite{tam2024letmespeak} despite degradation in other domains~\cite{banerjee2025crane,formattax2026}.
21	
```

</details>

54	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1, llama_scierc_full_s1, phase2_docred_full_s1, llama_docred_full_s1, mistral_scierc, mistral_docred
55	\paragraph{Results.}
56	RV and ET reach $\leq$0.03\% for all models on both datasets.
57	DR drops 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED; R-F1 improves in 5/6 settings.
58	CF direction depends on calibration: S1 introduces CF for calibrated models (up to 12.02\%; \S\ref{sec:discussion}) but reduces it for Mistral (56.87\%\,$\to$\,1.27\%); fine-tuning resolves both (\S\ref{sec:composability}).
59	Post-hoc filtering achieves DR${=}$0\% but at 15--74\% rejection rates; under fine-tuning, both strategies achieve comparable F1 (Appendix~\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).

8	DocRED (96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$, 14$\times$ SciERC scale) validates the pattern under a larger ontology (Table~\ref{tab:docred-ablation}).
9	S1 reduces DR from 18.46\% to 10.21\% ($\downarrow$45\%), eliminates RV (29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%) and ET, with R-F1 $+$0.50\,pp ($p < 0.001$).
10	The lower DR reduction (45\% vs.\ 94\% on SciERC) is consistent with higher $\bar{\kappa}$ (descriptive; \S\ref{sec:composition}): tighter constraints trigger more grammar-first fallback (\S\ref{sec:greedy-limitation}).
11	Grammar-only \emph{increases} DR (18.46\%\,$\to$\,26.17\%) by exposing latent violations when OOV relations become evaluable---a distinct cross-dataset confirmation of the enabling dependency.
12	Fine-tuning resolves residual DR entirely (Table~\ref{tab:composability}; residual decomposition in Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).
13

```
Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).
$\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\ unconstrained; Tot.\ = DR + CF; bold = best (CF excluded). F1 $\times$100.
S1 = single-pass, S2 = two-pass (\S\ref{sec:strategies}). Grammar = GCD ceiling~\cite{geng2023gcd}.

Grammar-first fallback activates on 0.5–5.7% of type-bearing positions during zero-shot decoding (correlating with $\bar{\kappa}$), but fine-tuning nearly eliminates it ($\leq$0.11%), shifting the semantic layer from primary enforcer to safety net; causal tracing shows all residual DR concentrates exclusively at fallback positions (Appendix~\ref{app:causal-tracing}).

S1 introduces calibration-dependent CF behavior: it amplifies CF for well-calibrated models (up to 12.02%) but suppresses it for Mistral (56.87%→1.27%), though fine-tuning resolves both patterns. Post-hoc filtering achieves perfect DR but requires rejecting 15–74% of inputs; under fine-tuning, both filtering and fine-tuning strategies reach comparable F1 scores (Appendix~\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).

For SciERC, S1 dramatically reduces detection rate from 15.37% to 0.93%—a 94% improvement—while maintaining statistical equivalence in recall-F1 ($p=0.58$), validating the empirical enabling dependency where grammar-only eliminates certain violations but achieves minimal DR reduction, semantic-only reaches 24%, and their composition yields the full 94% gain (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}). S1/S2 = single/two-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}).\nGrammar = GCD ceiling~\\cite{geng2023gcd}.\n$\\dagger$vLLM \\texttt{guided\\_json}~\\cite{kwon2023vllm} (\\S\\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}).}",
    "originalFile": "% Section 5 — SciERC Results (Draft)\n% Data source: Phase 2 SciERC registry experiments\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_grammar_only,\n%          phase2_scierc_semantic_only, phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_scierc_full_s2\n% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.\n\n\\subsection{Ablation and Composability with Fine-Tuning}\n\\label{sec:scierc-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} presents the five-way ablation on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 551 documents); only the active constraint layers differ across rows.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: SciERC five-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{6}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-7} \\cmidrule(l){8-9}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF & Tot.\\,$\\downarrow$\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 15.37 & ---\n  & 1.39  & 0.40\n  & 0.08  & 15.45\n  & 8.23  & 31.01 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 14.84 & $\\downarrow$3\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.08  & 14.92\n  & 8.23  & 30.98 \\\\\nGCD-equiv.$^\\dagger$\n  & 15.26 & $\\downarrow$1\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.04  & 15.30\n  & 8.75  & 30.91 \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 11.65 & $\\downarrow$24\\%\n  & 1.51  & 0.40\n  & 2.71  & 14.36\n  & 8.15  & 31.01 \\\\\nS1\n  & \\textbf{0.93} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{94\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 9.90  & \\textbf{10.83}\n  & \\textbf{8.36} & 30.98 \\\\\nS2\n  & 0.97  & $\\downarrow$94\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{9.00}\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{31.90} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ unconstrained.\nTot.\\ = DR + CF (total type-violation burden).\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded; Tot.\\ included).\nF1 $\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.\nCF: same entity assigned conflicting types across triples.\nS1 = single-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}), S2 = two-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:s2}).\nGrammar-only represents the ceiling of grammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}: vocabulary membership is enforced but cross-field semantic constraints are not.\n$\\dagger$GCD-equiv.: vLLM \\texttt{guided\\_json}~\\cite{kwon2023vllm}, an independent grammar-constrained implementation confirming Grammar row equivalence (\\S\\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}).}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nS1 reduces DR from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) with unchanged R-F1 ($p{=}0.58$), confirming the \\emph{empirical enabling dependency}: grammar-only eliminates RV/ET but achieves only 3\\% DR reduction; semantic-only reaches 24\\%; their composition yields 94\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).\nTotal type-violation burden decreases from 15.45\\% to 10.83\\%, reflecting \\emph{violation conversion} (CF 0.08\\%\\,$\\to$\\,9.90\\%) that fine-tuning resolves (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nS2 achieves comparable DR but lower R-F1; we adopt S1.\nPer-relation patterns and post-hoc comparison in Appendices~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape},~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\n% exp_ids: bootstrap_ci_ft_composability.json, w5_lora_scierc_*, w8_*\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% with no significant $\\Delta$R-F1 after Bonferroni correction (min.\\ $p{=}0.014$; 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.76, {+}0.22]$\\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\\% false positives; $\\leq$1.1\\% of true positives are affected across four fine-tuned configurations, with recall cost $\\leq$0.51\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream-impact}).\nA four-way ablation on fine-tuned models (Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}) reveals a qualitative shift: the grammar layer becomes a no-op (RV/ET already 0\\%; DR change $<$0.2\\,pp), making the semantic layer the sole effective DR defense (39--69\\% independent reduction).\nOn SciERC, three relations with $\\kappa \\geq 0.36$ retain DR under fine-tuning alone (\\textsc{Evaluate-for}: 13.1\\%, \\textsc{Part-of}: 5.8\\%, \\textsc{Compare}: 5.0\\%); S1 eliminates all residual violations across all seven relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:lora-per-relation}).\nCF drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X type ambiguity (63.3\\% of CF violations; Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) with LoRA eliminates all four violation types on both RE datasets (R-F1 43--47; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.56 & 0.08 & 40.89 & 82.35 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & \\textbf{0.00} & 0.22 & 40.79 & 81.87 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 44.55 & 84.77 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.00 & 0.26 & 44.70 & 84.71 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nF1\\,$\\times$100. No $\\Delta$R-F1 is significant (Bonferroni-corrected; details in text); all DR reductions are ($p{<}0.001$).\nE-F1 includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF: 7--8\\%; Llama PF: 2--3\\%).}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (f) Downstream impact of DR elimination\n%  exp_ids: task_e_lucky_violation_analysis\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Downstream impact.}\n\\label{sec:downstream-impact}\nAmong constraint-removed triples, only 0.49\\% match gold labels (``lucky violations''; Appendix~\\ref{app:lucky-violations}).\nError analysis of 514 false negatives (Qwen3-8B LoRA + S1) confirms only one (0.2\\%) was blocked by constraints; the dominant error sources---relation type confusion (Part-of$\\leftrightarrow$Used-for) and entity type polysemy---are schema-valid and beyond the reach of type constraints (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}).\nAcross all fine-tuned settings, only 36 residual DR violations remain, decomposing into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and relation/direction errors (28\\%); the irreducible floor from coverage gaps is $\\sim$3 violations (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nWe provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}\nXGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and related systems~\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking.\nNeuroLogic Decoding~\\cite{lu2021neurologic} enforces predicate-logic constraints via beam-level reranking; OntoCoDec applies constraints per-token via forward masking, avoiding beam-width dependence.\nIn code generation, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard} and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata; PICARD prunes invalid beam hypotheses post-generation, whereas OntoCoDec applies proactive token-level masking before sampling, and maintains a dynamic entity registry across multi-relation outputs rather than single-output SQL queries (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\nDCCD~\\cite{dccd2026} and \\citet{nguyen2026thinking} decouple reasoning from constrained generation via multi-pass architectures; OntoCoDec enforces schema semantics within a single forward pass.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}\nGoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, GUIDEX~\\cite{guidex2025}, GenIE~\\cite{josifoski2022genie}, TANL~\\cite{paolini2021tanl}, REBEL~\\cite{cabot2021rebel}, InstructUIE~\\cite{wang2023instructuie}, and others~\\cite{fei2022lasuie,wang2022deepstruct,lu2022uie,lou2023usm,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure or frame IE as structured generation, though schema compliance gaps persist~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.\nGoLLIE and KnowCoder embed schema knowledge via code-style prompts (training-time internalization without runtime enforcement); GoLLIE-7B eliminates DR at severe coverage cost (R-F1${=}$0.0, 46\\% empty output), while OntoCoDec achieves R-F1${=}$45.47 at DR${=}$0.09\\%~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}\n\\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \\citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives.\nEnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} uses ASP; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} backtracks on violations---OntoCoDec uses forward-only masking at $O(|V|)$ per step, avoiding backtracking latency.\nGrammar-only enforcement (GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only 3\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}).\nExtraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak} despite degradation in other domains~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,formattax2026}.\n",

    "originalFile": "% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)\n\nAt each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \\subseteq V$ and $S(t) \\subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.\nThe final mask is their conjunction:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:composition}\n  M_{\\text{final}}(t) = M_{\\text{grammar}}(t) \\;\\wedge\\; M_{\\text{semantic}}(t),\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $\\wedge$ denotes element-wise AND over the vocabulary $V$; a token is allowed only if it satisfies \\emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.\nThe conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$. This reflects the empirical enabling dependency (\\S\\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\\mathcal{R}$, a property enforced only by the grammar layer.\nThis dependency is scale-sensitive: 70B semantic-only achieves 84\\% DR reduction (vs.\\ 24\\% on 8B), though grammar remains necessary for RV elimination (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\\\"{\\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.\nWe enforce a \\emph{grammar-first fallback}: semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.\nStructural integrity is a hard constraint; type violations are soft errors detectable by downstream validation, with DR (ontologically invalid type pair) more severe than CF (individually valid triples with cross-triple type inconsistency).\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}\n\\label{eq:tightness}\nWe define per-relation constraint tightness $\\kappa(r) = 1 - |\\sigma(r)|/|\\mathcal{E}|^2$ and the schema-level mean $\\bar{\\kappa} = \\frac{1}{|\\mathcal{R}|}\\sum_{r} \\kappa(r)$.\nAcross three schemas---WikiEvents ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.029$), SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$), DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$)---DR reduction ranges from 94--99\\% (low $\\bar{\\kappa}$) to 42--90\\% (high $\\bar{\\kappa}$), though this correlation is descriptive only ($n{=}3$, $p{=}0.138$); entity type confusability is a critical co-factor (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa-case-study},~\\ref{app:tcr}); high-$\\bar{\\kappa}$ relations account for only 3.8\\% of gold triples in DocRED (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa-analysis}).\n\n\\paragraph{Greedy limitation and fallback cascade.}\n\\label{sec:greedy-limitation}\nThe semantic layer commits entity types greedily---an instance of Level~3 (non-local) constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability})---so CF violations arise when the same entity receives different types across triples; beam search mitigates this in favorable configurations (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nGrammar-first fallback triggers on 0.5--5.7\\% of type-bearing positions under zero-shot decoding, correlating with $\\bar{\\kappa}$; fine-tuning virtually eliminates fallback ($\\leq$\\,0.11\\%), transitioning the semantic layer from primary enforcer to safety net.\nCausal tracing confirms all residual DR occurs exclusively at fallback positions (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-rectangularity and independent enforcement.}\n\\label{sec:rectangularity}\nType signatures are non-rectangular (0/7 SciERC, 23/96 DocRED rectangular); independent enforcement is a sound over-approximation admitting 33--37\\% spurious pairs; fine-tuning eliminates all residual DR (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nSchema enumeration order does not affect decoding: shuffling entity and relation type order in the compiled constraints produces byte-identical outputs across all 551 SciERC documents, confirming that OntoCoDec is fully order-invariant (Appendix~\\ref{app:order-invariance}).\n",

we adopt S1.\nPer-relation patterns and post-hoc comparison in Appendices~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape},~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\n% exp_ids: bootstrap_ci_ft_composability.json, w5_lora_scierc_*, w8_*\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% with no significant $\\Delta$R-F1 after Bonferroni correction (min.\\ $p{=}0.014$; 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.76, {+}0.22]$\\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\\% false positives; $\\leq$1.1\\% of true positives are affected across four fine-tuned configurations, with recall cost $\\leq$0.51\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream-impact}).\nA four-way ablation on fine-tuned models (Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}) reveals a qualitative shift: the grammar layer becomes a no-op (RV/ET already 0\\%; DR change $<$0.2\\,pp), making the semantic layer the sole effective DR defense (39--69\\% independent reduction).\nOn SciERC, three relations with $\\kappa \\geq 0.36$ retain DR under fine-tuning alone (\\textsc{Evaluate-for}: 13.1\\%, \\textsc{Part-of}: 5.8\\%, \\textsc{Compare}: 5.0\\%); S1 eliminates all residual violations across all seven relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:lora-per-relation}).\nCF drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X type ambiguity (63.3\\% of CF violations; - §7 Conclusion/Future Work 标记 global entity-level type consensus 为最重要 future work

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BELGXJPcte3NKvyU3xZzsW`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.
3	A decidability analysis yields a two-layer architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
4	The two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1, scale-sensitive; \S\ref{sec:70b-results}): neither achieves full DR reduction alone.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning on three 8B-class models across two RE datasets (up to 96 relation types), \textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR ($<$\,0.1\%) at R-F1 41--46\% ($\pm$0.8\,pp); residual cross-field violations (CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\% under fine-tuning) represent \emph{violation conversion}; beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all four violation types under fine-tuning on both RE datasets (1.54$\times$ latency; \S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
7	Zero-shot evaluation serves as a diagnostic probe, revealing DR reduction of 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED across three models; constraint tightness ($\bar{\kappa}$) is a descriptive correlate of enforcement difficulty ($n{=}3$ schemas, $p{=}0.138$).
8	Preliminary 70B-class validation (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}) and event extraction on WikiEvents (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}) confirm generalizability beyond the primary 8B evaluation.
9	\end{abstract}
10	
```

  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "6525db06-8acd-4d51-871a-aed395f29a36",
  "type": "user",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "type": "text",
    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex",
      "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.\nA decidability analysis yields a two-layer architecture: a \\emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \\emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\nThe two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1, scale-sensitive; \\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}): neither achieves full DR reduction alone.\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nComposed with LoRA fine-tuning on three 8B-class models across two RE datasets (up to 96 relation types), \\textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR ($<$\\,0.1\\%) at R-F1 41--46\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp); residual cross-field violations (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% under fine-tuning) represent \\emph{violation conversion}; beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all four violation types under fine-tuning on both RE datasets (1.54$\\times$ latency; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nZero-shot evaluation serves as a diagnostic probe, revealing DR reduction of 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED across three models; constraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) is a descriptive correlate of enforcement difficulty ($n{=}3$ schemas, $p{=}0.138$).\nPreliminary 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}) and event extraction on WikiEvents (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}) confirm generalizability beyond the primary 8B evaluation.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
      "numLines": 10,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 10
    }
  },

1	% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion
2	
3	OntoCoDec translates IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints.
4	LoRA + greedy S1 near-eliminates DR ($<$\,0.1\%) with CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on both RE datasets (1.54$\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 42--99\% across three 8B-class models.
5	The central insight is that IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1): the semantic layer's entity registry provides random-access type lookups and forward masking~\cite{scholak2021picard,khorshidi2025odke}, bridging the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\% vs.\ 94\% DR reduction); this enabling dependency---scale-sensitive (\S\ref{sec:70b-results})---shows grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but insufficient for IE schema compliance.
6	Residual CF arises from per-triple independent enforcement; beam search resolves this (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
7

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QEyaYFHdhyafVmJPFJ3ypv`</summary>

```text
83-\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}
84-Zero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\% compared to $\sim$45\% for fine-tuned systems.
85-Zero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).
86:Scaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern may not generalize to other scales.
87-Composability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%, R-F1 within $\pm$0.8\,pp; EE: RoV$\to$0\%, AC$\leq$0.8\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\%, Llama 0.46\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.
88-WikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).
89-All main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.
```

```text
83	\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}
84	Zero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\% compared to $\sim$45\% for fine-tuned systems.
85	Zero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).
86	Scaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern may not generalize to other scales.
87	Composability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%, R-F1 within $\pm$0.8\,pp; EE: RoV$\to$0\%, AC$\leq$0.8\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\%, Llama 0.46\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.
88	WikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).
89	All main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.
90	
91	\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}
92	Scalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\,s; Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.\nA decidability analysis yields a two-layer architecture: a \\emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \\emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\nThe two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1, scale-sensitive; \\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}): neither achieves full DR reduction alone.\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nComposed with LoRA fine-tuning on three 8B-class models across two RE datasets (up to 96 relation types), \\textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR ($<$\\,0.1\\%) at R-F1 41--46\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp); residual cross-field violations (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% under fine-tuning) represent \\emph{violation conversion}; beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all four violation types under fine-tuning on both RE datasets (1.54$\\times$ latency; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nZero-shot evaluation serves as a diagnostic probe, revealing DR reduction of 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED across three models; constraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) is a descriptive correlate of enforcement difficulty ($n{=}3$ schemas, $p{=}0.138$).\nPreliminary 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}) and event extraction on WikiEvents (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}) confirm generalizability beyond the primary 8B evaluation.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

DR\\,$\\leq$\\,1\\% under greedy constrained decoding), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.27\\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.\nCF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\\,pp). CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.\nCF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.\nEntity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\\,pp for Qwen vs.\\ $-$0.64\\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\\% vs.\\ 6.8\\%).\nOn 70B DocRED LoRA, parse failure rises modestly under S1 (10.0\\%\\,$\\to$\\,12.5\\%) due to generation timeouts on long documents rather than JSON format errors; SciERC (shorter documents) shows 0\\% parse failure in both settings.\nResidual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\nSchema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) arise because RE constraints are extracted from training-data type pairs, which may not cover all valid combinations; larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.\nPost-hoc type consensus re-voting (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}) reduces CF violations by 18--92\\% depending on model and dataset, confirming that first-assignment ordering sensitivity is a primary CF source; however, integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}\nZero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\\% compared to $\\sim$45\\% for fine-tuned systems.\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern may not generalize to other scales.\nComposability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.8\\,pp; EE: RoV$\\to$0\\%, AC$\\leq$0.8\\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\\%, Llama 0.46\\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.\nWikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nScalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\\,s; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.\nOur approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.\nContinuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.\nThis assumption holds for our evaluation ontologies but may be challenged by ontologies with morphologically similar type names (e.g., \\texttt{Protein} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Complex} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Domain}); when type names share a common prefix longer than one token, masking granularity depends on the tokenizer's subword segmentation.\n70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may alter logit distributions at mask boundaries; the impact on constraint enforcement precision has not been isolated.\nOur evaluation is limited to English-language datasets.\nThe grammar layer (CFG compilation via XGrammar) is tokenizer-agnostic, applying unchanged to any BPE or SentencePiece tokenizer including those trained on CJK-heavy corpora.\nThe semantic layer's continuation masking is likewise language-independent in mechanism: prefix matching over type name tokens operates identically regardless of script, though CJK entity names produce more fragmented tokenizations under byte-level BPE (2--3$\\times$ more tokens per entity than English equivalents), increasing the number of masking steps per type field without affecting correctness.\nValidation on Chinese IE benchmarks (e.g., DuIE, CLUENER) remains future work.\nFine-tuning composability was validated exclusively with LoRA; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_power_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\\input{appendix_downstream_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_scalability}\n\\input{appendix_full_ft}\n\\input{appendix_tcr}\n\\input{appendix_kappa_case_study}\n\\input{appendix_high_kappa_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_order_invariance}\n\\input{appendix_pumping_lemma}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

{

  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section7_conclusion_compressed.tex",
  "old_string": "OntoCoDec translates IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints.\nLoRA + greedy S1 near-eliminates DR ($<$\\,0.1\\%) with CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on both RE datasets (1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 42--99\\% across three 8B-class models.\nThe central insight is that IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1): the semantic layer's entity registry provides random-access type lookups and forward masking~\\cite{scholak2021picard,khorshidi2025odke}, bridging the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\\% vs.\\ 94\\% DR reduction); this enabling dependency---scale-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results})---shows grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but insufficient for IE schema compliance.\nResidual CF arises from per-triple independent enforcement; beam search resolves this (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).",
  "new_string": "OntoCoDec translates IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, providing a systematic empirical framework---automatically compiled from ontologies, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks---rather than a novel decoding algorithm.\nLoRA + greedy S1 near-eliminates DR ($<$\\,0.1\\%) with CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on both RE datasets (1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 42--99\\% across three 8B-class models.\nThe central insight is that IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1): unlike rejection-based approaches that prune invalid outputs post-generation~\\cite{scholak2021picard}, the semantic layer applies constructive forward masking via a dynamic entity registry, bridging the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\\% vs.\\ 94\\% DR reduction); this enabling dependency---scale-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results})---shows grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but insufficient for IE schema compliance.\nResidual CF arises from per-triple independent enforcement; extending to global entity-level type consensus during decoding---eliminating CF without beam search overhead---is the most important direction for future work."
}

    "oldString": "OntoCoDec translates IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints.\nLoRA + greedy S1 near-eliminates DR ($<$\\,0.1\\%) with CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on both RE datasets (1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 42--99\\% across three 8B-class models.\nThe central insight is that IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1): the semantic layer's entity registry provides random-access type lookups and forward masking~\\cite{scholak2021picard,khorshidi2025odke}, bridging the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\\% vs.\\ 94\\% DR reduction); this enabling dependency---scale-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results})---shows grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but insufficient for IE schema compliance.\nResidual CF arises from per-triple independent enforcement; beam search resolves this (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).",
    "newString": "OntoCoDec translates IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, providing a systematic empirical framework---automatically compiled from ontologies, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks---rather than a novel decoding algorithm.\nLoRA + greedy S1 near-eliminates DR ($<$\\,0.1\\%) with CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on both RE datasets (1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 42--99\\% across three 8B-class models.\nThe central insight is that IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1): unlike rejection-based approaches that prune invalid outputs post-generation~\\cite{scholak2021picard}, the semantic layer applies constructive forward masking via a dynamic entity registry, bridging the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\\% vs.\\ 94\\% DR reduction); this enabling dependency---scale-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results})---shows grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but insufficient for IE schema compliance.\nResidual CF arises from per-triple independent enforcement; extending to global entity-level type consensus during decoding---eliminating CF without beam search overhead---is the most important direction for future work.",

  "old_string": "DocRED (96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$, 14$\\times$ SciERC scale) validates the pattern under a larger ontology (Table~\\ref{tab:docred-ablation}).\nS1 reduces DR from 18.46\\% to 10.21\\% ($\\downarrow$45\\%), eliminates RV (29.66\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.03\\%) and ET, with R-F1 $+$0.50\\,pp ($p < 0.001$).\nThe lower DR reduction (45\\% vs.\\ 94\\% on SciERC) is consistent with higher $\\bar{\\kappa}$ (descriptive; \\S\\ref{sec:composition}): tighter constraints trigger more grammar-first fallback (\\S\\ref{sec:greedy-limitation}).",

| Config | DR% | RV% | ET% | CF | R-F1 | E-F1 |
|--------|-----|-----|-----|-----|------|------|
| Unconstrained | 1.5 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 44.73 | 64.86 |
| Grammar-only | 1.5 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 44.63 | 64.82 |
| Semantic-only | 0.6 | 0 | 0 | 9 | 44.73 | 64.86 |
| Full S1 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 15 | 44.53 | 64.76 |
| Full S2 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 18 | 36.28 | 62.35 |
| Beam4 S1 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 1 | 44.81 | 64.89 |

**任务：**

1. 在 appendix 创建一个新表 "70B-Class (Qwen2.5-72B-AWQ) Ablation on SciERC"，包含以上 6 行数据。表格格式与主文 Table 1 一致。

2. 表下方写 2-3 句分析段落，覆盖以下要点：
   - Grammar layer 零贡献（DR 1.5%→1.5%），与 §5.5 ZS pattern 一致

17	A four-way ablation on SciERC reveals scale-dependent layer interaction: semantic-only achieves 84\% DR reduction (6.41\%\,$\to$\,1.01\%), far exceeding the 8B result (24\%; Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}), reflecting stronger internalized JSON generation; grammar remains necessary for RV elimination (0.61\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and CF control (semantic-only CF${=}$76).
18	% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained

20

```text
83	\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}
84	Zero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\% compared to $\sim$45\% for fine-tuned systems.
85	Zero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).
86	Scaling to smaller models has not been verified; the observed relationship between schema restrictiveness and enforcement difficulty is based on only three schemas.
87	Composability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%, R-F1 within $\pm$0.8\,pp; EE: RoV$\to$0\%, AC$\leq$0.8\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\%, Llama 0.46\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.
88	WikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).
89	All main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.
90	
91	\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}
92	Scalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\,s; Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.
93	Our approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.
94	Continuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.
95	This assumption holds for our evaluation ontologies but may be challenged by ontologies with morphologically similar type names (e.g., \texttt{Protein} vs.\ \texttt{Protein\_Complex} vs.\ \texttt{Protein\_Domain}); when type names share a common prefix longer than one token, masking granularity depends on the tokenizer's subword segmentation.
96	70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may alter logit distributions at mask boundaries; the impact on constraint enforcement precision has not been isolated.
97	Our evaluation is limited to English-language datasets.
98	The grammar layer (CFG compilation via XGrammar) is tokenizer-agnostic, applying unchanged to any BPE or SentencePiece tokenizer including those trained on CJK-heavy corpora.
99	The semantic layer's continuation masking is likewise language-independent in mechanism: prefix matching over type name tokens operates identically regardless of script, though CJK entity names produce more fragmented tokenizations under byte-level BPE (2--3$\times$ more tokens per entity than English equivalents), increasing the number of masking steps per type field without affecting correctness.
100	Validation on Chinese IE benchmarks (e.g., DuIE, CLUENER) remains future work.
101	Fine-tuning composability was validated exclusively with LoRA; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\%\,$\to$\,0\%; Appendix~\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.
102	
```

fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified; the observed relationship between schema restrictiveness and enforcement difficulty is based on only three schemas.\nComposability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.8\\,pp; EE: RoV$\\to$0\\%, AC$\\leq$0.8\\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\\%, Llama 0.46\\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.\nWikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nScalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\\,s; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.\nOur approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.\nContinuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.\nThis assumption holds for our evaluation ontologies but may be challenged by ontologies with morphologically similar type names (e.g., \\texttt{Protein} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Complex} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Domain}); when type names share a common prefix longer than one token, masking granularity depends on the tokenizer's subword segmentation.\n70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may alter logit distributions at mask boundaries; the impact on constraint enforcement precision has not been isolated.\nOur evaluation is limited to English-language datasets.\nThe grammar layer (CFG compilation via XGrammar) is tokenizer-agnostic, applying unchanged to any BPE or SentencePiece tokenizer including those trained on CJK-heavy corpora.\nThe semantic layer's continuation masking is likewise language-independent in mechanism: prefix matching over type name tokens operates identically regardless of script, though CJK entity names produce more fragmented tokenizations under byte-level BPE (2--3$\\times$ more tokens per entity than English equivalents), increasing the number of masking steps per type field without affecting correctness.\nValidation on Chinese IE benchmarks (e.g., DuIE, CLUENER) remains future work.\nFine-tuning composability was validated exclusively with LoRA; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.\n",

DR\\,$\\leq$\\,1\\% under greedy constrained decoding), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.27\\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.\nCF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\\,pp). CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.\nCF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.\nEntity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\\,pp for Qwen vs.\\ $-$0.64\\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\\% vs.\\ 6.8\\%).\nOn 70B DocRED LoRA, parse failure rises modestly under S1 (10.0\\%\\,$\\to$\\,12.5\\%) due to generation timeouts on long documents rather than JSON format errors; SciERC (shorter documents) shows 0\\% parse failure in both settings.\nResidual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\nSchema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) arise because RE constraints are extracted from training-data type pairs, which may not cover all valid combinations; larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.\nPost-hoc type consensus re-voting (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}) reduces CF violations by 18--92\\% depending on model and dataset, confirming that first-assignment ordering sensitivity is a primary CF source; however, integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}\nZero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\\% compared to $\\sim$45\\% for fine-tuned systems.\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified; the observed relationship between schema restrictiveness and enforcement difficulty is based on only three schemas.\nComposability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.8\\,pp; EE: RoV$\\to$0\\%, AC$\\leq$0.8\\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\\%, Llama 0.46\\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.\nWikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nScalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\\,s; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.\nOur approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.\nContinuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.\nThis assumption holds for our evaluation ontologies but may be challenged by ontologies with morphologically similar type names (e.g., \\texttt{Protein} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Complex} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Domain}); when type names share a common prefix longer than one token, masking granularity depends on the tokenizer's subword segmentation.\n70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may alter logit distributions at mask boundaries; the impact on constraint enforcement precision has not been isolated.\nOur evaluation is limited to English-language datasets.\nThe grammar layer (CFG compilation via XGrammar) is tokenizer-agnostic, applying unchanged to any BPE or SentencePiece tokenizer including those trained on CJK-heavy corpora.\nThe semantic layer's continuation masking is likewise language-independent in mechanism: prefix matching over type name tokens operates identically regardless of script, though CJK entity names produce more fragmented tokenizations under byte-level BPE (2--3$\\times$ more tokens per entity than English equivalents), increasing the number of masking steps per type field without affecting correctness.\nValidation on Chinese IE benchmarks (e.g., DuIE, CLUENER) remains future work.\nFine-tuning composability was validated exclusively with LoRA; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_power_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\\input{appendix_downstream_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_scalability}\n\\input{appendix_full_ft}\n\\input{appendix_tcr}\n\\input{appendix_kappa_case_study}\n\\input{appendix_high_kappa_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_order_invariance}\n\\input{appendix_pumping_lemma}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_70b_ablation}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_power_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\\input{appendix_downstream_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_scalability}\n\\input{appendix_full_ft}\n\\input{appendix_tcr}\n\\input{appendix_kappa_case_study}\n\\input{appendix_high_kappa_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_order_invariance}\n\\input{appendix_pumping_lemma}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

    "originalFile": "% Appendix: 70B-Class LoRA Ablation on SciERC\n% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_unconstrained, eval_70b_lora_scierc_grammar_only,\n%          eval_70b_lora_scierc_semantic_only, eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained,\n%          eval_70b_lora_scierc_s2_constrained, eval_70b_lora_scierc_beam4_s1\n\n\\paragraph{70B-class fine-tuned ablation.}\n\\label{app:70b-ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:70b-ft-ablation} presents the constraint ablation on Qwen2.5-72B-AWQ with LoRA fine-tuning on SciERC (551 documents).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{RV\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{ET\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained & 1.50 & 0 & 0 & 0    & 44.73 & 64.86 \\\\\nGrammar       & 1.50 & 0 & 0 & 0    & 44.63 & 64.82 \\\\\nSemantic      & 0.60 & 0 & 0 & 9    & 44.73 & 64.86 \\\\\nS1            & \\textbf{0} & 0 & 0 & 15 & 44.53 & 64.76 \\\\\nS2            & \\textbf{0} & 0 & 0 & 18 & 36.28 & 62.35 \\\\\nBeam4 S1      & \\textbf{0} & 0 & 0 & \\textbf{1} & \\textbf{44.81} & \\textbf{64.89} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{70B-class (Qwen2.5-72B-AWQ) LoRA ablation on SciERC (551 docs).\nCF: violation count. F1 $\\times$100.}\n\\label{tab:70b-ft-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe grammar layer provides zero DR reduction (1.50\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.50\\%), consistent with the zero-shot 70B pattern where fine-tuned models already produce structurally valid JSON (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nThe semantic layer is the sole effective defense (1.50\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.60\\%, 60\\% reduction) with no R-F1 cost ($\\Delta{=}$0\\,pp), and full S1 eliminates all DR.\nS2 eliminates DR but causes severe R-F1 degradation ($-$8.45\\,pp), indicating that optimal constraint granularity is scale-dependent: the additional S2 constraints become overly restrictive at 70B, where stronger baseline generation makes fine-grained intervention counterproductive.\nBeam ($k{=}4$) S1 remains the optimal configuration (DR${=}$0\\%, CF${=}$1, R-F1${=}$44.81).\n",

    "originalFile": "% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)\n\n\\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}\n\\label{sec:runtime}\nS1 overhead is $+$13.1\\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\\% with 37\\% lower absolute latency.\nConstraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n\n\\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}\n\\label{sec:beam-results}\nWith LoRA, beam search ($k{=}4$)~\\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on both SciERC and DocRED (R-F1 43--47; Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}), yielding two deployment tiers: greedy S1 ($+$10\\% latency, DR${<}$0.1\\%, CF${\\leq}$1.4\\%) and beam S1 (1.54$\\times$ latency, all violations${\\to}$0\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:beam-latency}).\n\n% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200\n\\paragraph{Preliminary 70B-class validation.}\n\\label{sec:70b-results}\nOn Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4), zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets: SciERC 6.41\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 11.24), DocRED 14.98\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 13.05); CF rises to 0.04--3.50\\%, comparable to 8B zero-shot CF.\n% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_ablation (grammar_only, semantic_only, full_s1)\nA four-way ablation on SciERC reveals scale-dependent layer interaction: semantic-only achieves 84\\% DR reduction (6.41\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.01\\%), far exceeding the 8B result (24\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}), reflecting stronger internalized JSON generation; grammar remains necessary for RV elimination (0.61\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and CF control (semantic-only CF${=}$76).\n% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained\nLoRA confirms composability: DR\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% on both datasets (R-F1 44.53, 43.95; $|\\Delta| \\leq 0.20$\\,pp); CF drops to 0.18--1.33\\%, consistent with the 8B pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:composability}).\n\n% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc\n\\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}\n\\label{sec:constrained-baselines}\nOur grammar-only ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same constraints as GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}; vLLM \\texttt{guided\\_json}~\\cite{kwon2023vllm} confirms equivalence (DR${=}$15.26\\%, R-F1${=}$8.75; GCD-equiv.\\ row, Appendix~\\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).\nThe ${\\sim}$15\\% residual DR is the gap our semantic layer addresses (94\\% total reduction).\nTrie-only constraints without JSON enforcement yield 9--51\\% parse failure (Appendix~\\ref{app:trie-baseline}).\n",

33	
34	The grammar layer provides zero DR reduction (1.50\%\,$\to$\,1.50\%), consistent with the zero-shot 70B pattern where fine-tuned models already produce structurally valid JSON (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}).
35	The semantic layer is the sole effective defense (1.50\%\,$\to$\,0.60\%, 60\% reduction) with no R-F1 cost ($\Delta{=}$0\,pp), and full S1 eliminates all DR.
36	S2 eliminates DR but causes severe R-F1 degradation ($-$8.45\,pp), indicating that optimal constraint granularity is scale-dependent: the additional S2 constraints become overly restrictive at 70B, where stronger baseline generation makes fine-grained intervention counterproductive.
37	Beam ($k{=}4$) S1 remains the optimal configuration (DR${=}$0\%, CF${=}$1, R-F1${=}$44.81).
38
